**Alberta** is one of the 13 [provinces and territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_and_territories_of_Canada "Provinces and territories of Canada") of Canada. It is a part of [Western Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Canada "Western Canada") and is one of the three [prairie provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Prairies "Canadian Prairies"). Alberta borders [British Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Columbia "British Columbia") to the west, [Saskatchewan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saskatchewan "Saskatchewan") to the east, the [Northwest Territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Territories "Northwest Territories") to the north, and the [U.S. state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._state "U.S. state") of [Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana "Montana") to the south. It is one of the only two landlocked provinces in Canada, with Saskatchewan being the other.[\[8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:3-8) The eastern part of the province is occupied by the [Great Plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Plains "Great Plains"), while the western part borders the [Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountains "Rocky Mountains"). The province has a predominantly [continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate "Humid continental climate") but experiences quick temperature changes due to air [aridity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arid "Arid"). Seasonal temperature swings are less pronounced in western Alberta due to occasional [Chinook winds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinook_wind "Chinook wind").[\[9\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-9)

Alberta is the fourth-largest province by area at 661,848 square kilometres (255,541 square miles),[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-10) and the fourth-most populous, being home to 4,262,635 people.[\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Alberta-2) Alberta's capital is [Edmonton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmonton "Edmonton"), while [Calgary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calgary "Calgary") is its largest city.[\[11\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-11) The two are Alberta's largest [census metropolitan areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Census_geographic_units_of_Canada "Census geographic units of Canada").[\[12\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-StatCan2016CMAsCAs-12) More than half of Albertans live in either Edmonton or Calgary, which contributes to continuing the [rivalry between the two cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Alberta "Battle of Alberta"). [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language "English language") is the official language of the province. In 2016, 76.0% of Albertans were anglophone, 1.8% were [francophone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Albertans "Franco-Albertans") and 22.2% were [allophone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allophone_\(Canada\) "Allophone (Canada)").[\[13\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-13)

[Alberta's economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Alberta "Economy of Alberta") is based on [hydrocarbons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrocarbon "Hydrocarbon"), [petrochemical industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrochemical_industry "Petrochemical industry"), livestock and agriculture.[\[14\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-14) The [oil and gas industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleum_industry_in_Canada "Petroleum industry in Canada") has been a pillar of Alberta's economy since 1947, when substantial oil deposits were discovered at [Leduc No. 1 well.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leduc_No._1 "Leduc No. 1")[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alberta1947-15) It has also become a part of the province's identity. Since Alberta is the province most rich in hydrocarbons, it provides 70% of the oil and natural gas produced on Canadian soil. In 2018, Alberta's output was CA$338.2 billion, 15.27% of Canada's GDP.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-16)[\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-17)

Until the 1930s, [Alberta's political landscape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Alberta "Politics of Alberta") consisted of two major parties: the centre-left [Liberals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Liberal_Party "Alberta Liberal Party") and the [agrarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarianism "Agrarianism") [United Farmers of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Farmers_of_Alberta "United Farmers of Alberta"). Today, Alberta is generally perceived as a conservative province. The right-wing [Social Credit Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Social_Credit_Party "Alberta Social Credit Party") held office continually from 1935 to 1971 before the centre-right [Progressive Conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Association_of_Alberta "Progressive Conservative Association of Alberta") held office continually from 1971 to 2015, the latter being the longest unbroken run in government at the provincial or federal level in Canadian history.

Since before becoming part of Canada, Alberta has been home to several [First Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Nations_in_Alberta "First Nations in Alberta") like [Plains Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plains_Indians "Plains Indians") and [Woodland Cree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodland_Cree "Woodland Cree"). It was also a territory used by [fur traders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fur_trade "Fur trade") of the rival companies [Hudson's Bay Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson%27s_Bay_Company "Hudson's Bay Company") and [North West Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_West_Company "North West Company"). The Dominion of Canada bought the lands that would become Alberta as part of the NWT in 1870.[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-18) From the late 1800s to early 1900s, many immigrants arrived to prevent the prairies from being annexed by the United States. Growing wheat and cattle ranching also became very profitable. In 1905, the [Alberta Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Act "Alberta Act") was passed, creating the province of Alberta.[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Alberta_becomes_a_Province-19) Massive oil reserves were discovered in 1947. The exploitation of [oil sands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_sands "Oil sands") began in 1967.[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alberta1947-15)

Alberta is renowned for its natural beauty, richness in fossils and for housing important nature reserves. Alberta is home to six [UNESCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO "UNESCO")-designated [World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Site "World Heritage Site"): the [Canadian Rocky Mountain Parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Rocky_Mountain_Parks_World_Heritage_Site "Canadian Rocky Mountain Parks World Heritage Site"), [Dinosaur Provincial Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_Provincial_Park "Dinosaur Provincial Park"), [Head-Smashed-In Buffalo Jump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head-Smashed-In_Buffalo_Jump "Head-Smashed-In Buffalo Jump"), [Waterton-Glacier International Peace Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waterton-Glacier_International_Peace_Park "Waterton-Glacier International Peace Park"), [Wood Buffalo National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wood_Buffalo_National_Park "Wood Buffalo National Park") and [Writing-on-Stone Provincial Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writing-on-Stone_Provincial_Park "Writing-on-Stone Provincial Park").[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-20) Other popular sites include [Banff National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banff_National_Park "Banff National Park"), [Elk Island National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elk_Island_National_Park "Elk Island National Park"), [Jasper National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jasper_National_Park "Jasper National Park"), [Waterton Lakes National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waterton_Lakes_National_Park "Waterton Lakes National Park"), and [Drumheller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drumheller "Drumheller").

## Etymology

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=1 "Edit section: Etymology")]

Alberta was named after [Princess Louise Caroline Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princess_Louise,_Duchess_of_Argyll "Princess Louise, Duchess of Argyll") (1848–1939),[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-21) the fourth daughter of [Queen Victoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Victoria "Queen Victoria"). Princess Louise was the wife of [John Campbell, Marquess of Lorne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Campbell,_9th_Duke_of_Argyll "John Campbell, 9th Duke of Argyll"), [Governor General of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_General_of_Canada "Governor General of Canada") (1878–83). [Lake Louise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Louise_\(Alberta\) "Lake Louise (Alberta)") and [Mount Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Alberta "Mount Alberta") were also named in her honour.[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-22)[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-PlaceNames-23)

The name "Alberta" is a feminine [Latinized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latinisation_of_names "Latinisation of names") form of [Albert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_\(given_name\) "Albert (given name)"), the name of Princess Louise's father, the [Prince Consort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert,_Prince_Consort "Albert, Prince Consort") ([cf.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cf. "Cf.") [Medieval Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Latin_language "Medieval Latin language"): *[Albertus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albertus_\(given_name\) "Albertus (given name)")*, masculine) and its [Germanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_languages "Germanic languages") cognates, ultimately derived from the [Proto-Germanic language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Germanic_language "Proto-Germanic language") *\*Aþalaberhtaz* (compound of "noble" + "bright/famous").[\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-24)[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-25)

## Geography

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=2 "Edit section: Geography")]

Main article: [Geography of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Alberta "Geography of Alberta")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Alberta_Topo_Labeled_90_dpi.png/400px-Alberta_Topo_Labeled_90_dpi.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Alberta_Topo_Labeled_90_dpi.png)

A topographic map of Alberta, showing cities, towns, municipal district (county) and rural municipality borders, and natural features

Alberta, with an area of 661,848 square kilometres (255,541 square miles), is the fourth-largest province after [Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec "Quebec"), [Ontario](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ontario "Ontario"), and [British Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Columbia "British Columbia").[\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-area-26)

Alberta's southern border is the [49th parallel north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/49th_parallel_north "49th parallel north"), which [separates it](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_States_border "Canada–United States border") from the U.S. state of [Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana "Montana"). The [60th parallel north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/60th_parallel_north "60th parallel north") divides Alberta from the [Northwest Territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Territories "Northwest Territories"). The [110th meridian west](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/110th_meridian_west "110th meridian west") separates it from the province of [Saskatchewan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saskatchewan "Saskatchewan"); while on the west its boundary with British Columbia follows the [120th meridian west](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/120th_meridian_west "120th meridian west") south from the Northwest Territories at 60°N until it reaches the [Continental Divide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Divide_of_the_Americas "Continental Divide of the Americas") at the [Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountains "Rocky Mountains"), and from that point follows the line of peaks marking the Continental Divide in a generally southeasterly direction until it reaches the Montana border at 49°N.[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-27)

The province extends 1,223 kilometres (760 miles) north to south and 660 kilometres (410 miles) east to west at its maximum width. Its highest point is 3,747 metres (12,293 feet) at the summit of [Mount Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Columbia_\(Canada\) "Mount Columbia (Canada)") in the Rocky Mountains along the southwest border while its lowest point is 152 metres (499 feet) on the [Slave River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_River "Slave River") in [Wood Buffalo National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wood_Buffalo_National_Park "Wood Buffalo National Park") in the northeast.[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-aboutab-28)

With the exception of the [semi-arid climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-arid_climate "Semi-arid climate") of the [steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steppe "Steppe") in the south-eastern section, the province has adequate [water resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_resources "Water resources"). There are [numerous rivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_rivers_of_Alberta "List of rivers of Alberta") and [lakes in Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_lakes_of_Alberta "List of lakes of Alberta") used for swimming, fishing and a range of water sports. There are three large lakes, [Lake Claire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Claire_\(Alberta\) "Lake Claire (Alberta)") (1,436 km2 \[554 sq mi]) in Wood Buffalo National Park, [Lesser Slave Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesser_Slave_Lake "Lesser Slave Lake") (1,168 km2 \[451 sq mi]), and [Lake Athabasca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Athabasca "Lake Athabasca") (7,898 km2 \[3,049 sq mi]), which lies in both Alberta and Saskatchewan. The longest river in the province is the [Athabasca River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athabasca_River "Athabasca River"), which travels 1,538 km (956 mi) from the [Columbia Icefield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_Icefield "Columbia Icefield") in the Rocky Mountains to Lake Athabasca.[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-29)

The largest river is the [Peace River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_River "Peace River") with an average flow of 2,100 m3/s (74,000 cu ft/s).[\[30\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-30) The Peace River originates in the Rocky Mountains of northern British Columbia and flows through [northern Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Alberta "Northern Alberta") and into the Slave River, a tributary of the [Mackenzie River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mackenzie_River "Mackenzie River").

Alberta's capital city, [Edmonton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmonton "Edmonton"), is at about the geographic centre of the province. It is the most northerly major city in Canada and serves as a gateway and hub for resource development in northern Canada. With its proximity to Canada's largest oil fields, the region has most of western Canada's oil refinery capacity. Calgary is about 280 km (170 mi) south of Edmonton and 240 km (150 mi) north of Montana, surrounded by extensive ranching country. Almost 75% of the province's population lives in the [Calgary–Edmonton Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calgary%E2%80%93Edmonton_Corridor "Calgary–Edmonton Corridor"). The land grant policy to the railways served as a means to populate the province in its early years.[\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-31)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/1_moraine_lake_pano_2019.jpg/220px-1_moraine_lake_pano_2019.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:1_moraine_lake_pano_2019.jpg)

[Moraine Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moraine_Lake "Moraine Lake") at [Banff National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banff_National_Park "Banff National Park"). The [Alberta Mountain forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Mountain_forests "Alberta Mountain forests") makes up the southwestern boundary of Alberta.

Most of the northern half of the province is [boreal forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiga "Taiga"), while the Rocky Mountains along the southwestern boundary are largely [temperate coniferous forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_coniferous_forest "Temperate coniferous forest") of the [Alberta Mountain forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Mountain_forests "Alberta Mountain forests") and [Alberta–British Columbia foothills forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta%E2%80%93British_Columbia_foothills_forests "Alberta–British Columbia foothills forests"). The southern quarter of the province is [prairie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prairie "Prairie"), ranging from [shortgrass prairie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortgrass_prairie "Shortgrass prairie") in the southeastern corner to [mixed grass prairie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_grass_prairie "Mixed grass prairie") in an arc to the west and north of it. The central [aspen parkland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aspen_parkland "Aspen parkland") region extending in a broad arc between the prairies and the forests, from Calgary, north to Edmonton, and then east to [Lloydminster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lloydminster "Lloydminster"), contains the most [fertile soil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_fertility "Soil fertility") in the province and most of the population. Much of the unforested part of Alberta is given over either to grain farming or cattle ranching, with [mixed farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_farming "Mixed farming") more common in the north and centre, while [ranching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranch "Ranch") and [irrigated agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irrigation "Irrigation") predominate in the south.[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-32)

The Alberta [badlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badlands "Badlands") are in southeastern Alberta, where the [Red Deer River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Deer_River "Red Deer River") crosses the flat prairie and farmland, and features deep canyons and striking landforms. [Dinosaur Provincial Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_Provincial_Park "Dinosaur Provincial Park"), near [Brooks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooks,_Alberta "Brooks, Alberta"), showcases the badlands terrain, desert flora, and remnants from Alberta's past when dinosaurs roamed the then lush landscape.

### Climate

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=3 "Edit section: Climate")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Alberta_K%C3%B6ppen.svg/300px-Alberta_K%C3%B6ppen.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Alberta_K%C3%B6ppen.svg)

[Köppen climate types](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification "Köppen climate classification") in Alberta

Alberta extends for over 1,200 km (750 mi) from north to south; its climate, therefore, varies considerably. Average high temperatures in January range from 0 °C (32 °F) in the southwest to −24 °C (−11 °F) in the far north. The presence of the Rocky Mountains also influences the climate to the southwest, which disrupts the flow of the [prevailing westerly winds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westerlies "Westerlies") and causes them to drop most of their moisture on the western slopes of the mountain ranges before reaching the province, casting a [rain shadow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rain_shadow "Rain shadow") over much of Alberta. The northerly location and isolation from the weather systems of the Pacific Ocean cause Alberta to have a dry climate with little moderation from the ocean. Annual precipitation ranges from 300 mm (12 in) in the southeast to 450 mm (18 in) in the north, except in the foothills of the Rocky Mountains where total precipitation including snowfall can reach 600 mm (24 in) annually.[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-aboutab-28)[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-33)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Red_rock_coulee_at_sunset.jpg/220px-Red_rock_coulee_at_sunset.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Red_rock_coulee_at_sunset.jpg)

Southeastern Alberta features a [semi-arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-arid_climate "Semi-arid climate") [steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steppe "Steppe") climate.

Northern Alberta is mostly covered by boreal forest and has a [subarctic climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subarctic_climate "Subarctic climate"). The agricultural area of [southern Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Alberta "Southern Alberta") has a semi-arid steppe climate because the annual precipitation is less than the water that [evaporates or is used by plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evapotranspiration "Evapotranspiration"). The southeastern corner of Alberta, part of the [Palliser Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palliser%27s_Triangle "Palliser's Triangle"), experiences greater summer heat and lower rainfall than the rest of the province, and as a result, suffers frequent [crop yield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crop_yield "Crop yield") problems and occasional severe droughts. Western Alberta is protected by the mountains and enjoys the mild temperatures brought by winter Chinook winds. Central and parts of northwestern Alberta in the Peace River region are largely aspen parkland, a [biome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biome "Biome") transitional between prairie to the south and boreal forest to the north.

Alberta has a [humid continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate "Humid continental climate") with warm summers and cold winters. The province is open to cold Arctic weather systems from the north, which often produce cold winter conditions. As the [fronts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weather_front "Weather front") between the [air masses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_mass "Air mass") shift north and south across Alberta, the temperature can change rapidly. [Arctic air masses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_front "Arctic front") in the winter produce extreme minimum temperatures varying from −54 °C (−65 °F) in northern Alberta to −46 °C (−51 °F) in southern Alberta, although temperatures at these extremes are rare.

In the summer, continental air masses have produced record maximum temperatures from 32 °C (90 °F) in the mountains to over 40 °C (104 °F) in southeastern Alberta.[\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-climatlas-34) Alberta is a sunny province. Annual bright sunshine totals range between 1,900 up to just under 2,600 hours per year. Northern Alberta gets about 18 hours of daylight in the summer.[\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-climatlas-34) The average daytime temperatures range from around 21 °C (70 °F) in the [Rocky Mountain valleys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountains "Rocky Mountains") and far north, up to around 28 °C (82 °F) in the dry prairie of the southeast. The northern and western parts of the province experience higher rainfall and lower evaporation rates caused by cooler summer temperatures. The south and east-central portions are prone to drought-like conditions sometimes persisting for several years, although even these areas can receive heavy precipitation, sometimes resulting in flooding.

In the winter, the [Alberta clipper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_clipper "Alberta clipper"), a type of intense, fast-moving winter storm that generally forms over or near the province and, pushed with great speed by the continental polar [jetstream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_stream "Jet stream"), descends over the rest of southern Canada and the northern tier of the United States.[\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Alberta_Clipper-35) In southwestern Alberta, the cold winters are frequently interrupted by warm, dry [Chinook winds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinook_wind "Chinook wind") blowing from the mountains, which can propel temperatures upward from frigid conditions to well above the freezing point in a very short period. During one Chinook recorded at [Pincher Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pincher_Creek "Pincher Creek"), temperatures soared from −19 to 22 °C (−2 to 72 °F) in just one hour.[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-aboutab-28) The region around Lethbridge has the most Chinooks, averaging 30 to 35 Chinook days per year. Calgary has a 56% chance of a [white Christmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Christmas_\(weather\) "White Christmas (weather)"), while Edmonton has an 86% chance.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Canadawhitechristmas-36)

After Saskatchewan, Alberta experiences the most [tornadoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tornado "Tornado") in Canada with an average of 15 verified per year.[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-tornados-37) Thunderstorms, some of them severe, are frequent in the summer, especially in central and southern Alberta. The region surrounding the Calgary–Edmonton Corridor is notable for having the highest frequency of [hail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hail "Hail") in Canada, which is caused by [orographic lifting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orographic_lift "Orographic lift") from the nearby Rocky Mountains, enhancing the updraft/downdraft cycle necessary for the formation of hail.

### Ecology

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=4 "Edit section: Ecology")]

#### Flora

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=5 "Edit section: Flora")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Rosa_acicularis_8448.JPG/220px-Rosa_acicularis_8448.JPG)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rosa_acicularis_8448.JPG)

The [wild rose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosa_acicularis "Rosa acicularis") is the provincial flower of Alberta.

In central and northern Alberta the arrival of spring is marked by the early flowering of the [prairie crocus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulsatilla_nuttalliana "Pulsatilla nuttalliana") (*Pulsatilla nuttalliana*) *[anemone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anemone "Anemone")*; this member of the buttercup family has been recorded flowering as early as March, though April is the usual month for the general population.[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-40) Other prairie flora known to flower early are the [golden bean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermopsis_rhombifolia "Thermopsis rhombifolia") (*Thermopsis rhombifolia*) and [wild rose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosa_acicularis "Rosa acicularis") (*Rosa acicularis*).[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Jennings2010-41) Members of the [sunflower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helianthus "Helianthus") (*Helianthus*) family blossom on the prairie in the summer months between July and September.[\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Angier1974-42) The southern and east central parts of Alberta are covered by short prairie grass,[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Johnsgard2005-43) which dries up as summer lengthens, to be replaced by hardy perennials such as the [prairie coneflower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ratibida "Ratibida") (*Ratibida*), [fleabane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fleabane "Fleabane"), and [sage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagebrush "Sagebrush") (*[Artemisia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemisia_\(plant\) "Artemisia (plant)")*). Both yellow and white [sweet clover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melilotus "Melilotus") (*Melilotus*) can be found throughout the southern and central areas of the province.

The trees in the parkland region of the province grow in clumps and belts on the hillsides. These are largely [deciduous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deciduous "Deciduous"), typically [aspen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aspen "Aspen"), [poplar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populus "Populus"), and [willow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willow "Willow"). Many species of willow and other shrubs grow in virtually any terrain. North of the North Saskatchewan River, evergreen forests prevail for thousands of square kilometres. Aspen poplar, [balsam poplar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populus_balsamifera "Populus balsamifera") (*Populus balsamifera*) or in some parts [cottonwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populus_deltoides "Populus deltoides") (*Populus deltoides*), and [paper birch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betula_papyrifera "Betula papyrifera") (*Betula papyrifera*) are the primary large deciduous species. [Conifers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conifer "Conifer") include [jack pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_pine "Jack pine") (*Pinus banksiana*), Rocky Mountain pine, [lodgepole pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinus_contorta "Pinus contorta") (*Pinus contorta*), both white and black [spruce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spruce "Spruce"), and the deciduous conifer [tamarack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larix_laricina "Larix laricina") (*Larix laricina*).

#### Fauna

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=6 "Edit section: Fauna")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Bighorn_Sheep_-_Kananaskis.jpg/220px-Bighorn_Sheep_-_Kananaskis.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bighorn_Sheep_-_Kananaskis.jpg)

A [bighorn sheep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bighorn_sheep "Bighorn sheep") in [Kananaskis Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kananaskis_Country "Kananaskis Country"). The bighorn sheep is the provincial mammal of Alberta.

The four climatic regions ([alpine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_climate "Alpine climate"), [boreal forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiga "Taiga"), [parkland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aspen_parkland "Aspen parkland"), and [prairie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prairie "Prairie")) of Alberta are home to many different species of animals. The south and central prairie was the homeland of the [American bison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_bison "American bison"), also known as buffalo, with its grasses providing pasture and breeding ground for millions of buffalo. The buffalo population was decimated during early settlement, but since then, buffalo have made a comeback, living on farms and in parks all over Alberta.

[Herbivores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbivore "Herbivore") are found throughout the province. [Moose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moose "Moose"), [mule deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mule_deer "Mule deer"), [elk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elk "Elk"), and [white-tailed deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-tailed_deer "White-tailed deer") are found in the wooded regions, and [pronghorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pronghorn "Pronghorn") can be found in the prairies of southern Alberta. [Bighorn sheep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bighorn_sheep "Bighorn sheep") and [mountain goats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_goat "Mountain goat") live in the Rocky Mountains. Rabbits, [porcupines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porcupine "Porcupine"), [skunks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Striped_skunk "Striped skunk"), squirrels, and many species of rodents and reptiles live in every corner of the province. Alberta is home to only one venomous snake species, the [prairie rattlesnake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crotalus_viridis "Crotalus viridis").

Alberta is home to many large [carnivores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnivore "Carnivore") such as [wolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf "Wolf"), [grizzly bears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grizzly_bear "Grizzly bear"), [black bears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_black_bear "American black bear"), and [mountain lions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cougar "Cougar"), which are found in the mountains and wooded regions. Smaller carnivores of the [canine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canidae "Canidae") and [feline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felidae "Felidae") families include [coyotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coyote "Coyote"), [red foxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_fox "Red fox"), [Canada lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_lynx "Canada lynx"), and [bobcats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobcat "Bobcat"). [Wolverines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolverine "Wolverine") can also be found in the northwestern areas of the province.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/Alberta_Department_of_Public_Health_Rat_Poster_%2826497442131%29.jpg/220px-Alberta_Department_of_Public_Health_Rat_Poster_%2826497442131%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Alberta_Department_of_Public_Health_Rat_Poster_\(26497442131\).jpg)

Alberta Department of Public Health rat poster (1948)

Central and northern Alberta and the region farther north are the nesting ground of many migratory birds. Vast numbers of ducks, [geese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goose "Goose"), [swans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swan "Swan") and [pelicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pelican "Pelican") arrive in Alberta every spring and nest on or near one of the hundreds of small lakes that dot northern Alberta. [Eagles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eagle "Eagle"), [hawks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawk "Hawk"), owls, and [crows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crow "Crow") are plentiful, and a huge variety of smaller seed and insect-eating birds can be found. Alberta, like other [temperate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_climate "Temperate climate") regions, is home to [mosquitoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosquito "Mosquito"), [flies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fly "Fly"), [wasps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wasp "Wasp"), and bees. Rivers and lakes are populated with [pike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esox "Esox"), [walleye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walleye "Walleye"), [whitefish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freshwater_whitefish "Freshwater whitefish"), [rainbow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainbow_trout "Rainbow trout"), [speckled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brook_trout "Brook trout"), [brown trout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_trout "Brown trout"), and [sturgeon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sturgeon "Sturgeon"). Native to the province, the [bull trout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bull_trout "Bull trout"), is the provincial fish and an official [symbol of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbols_of_Alberta "Symbols of Alberta"). Turtles are found in some water bodies in the southern part of the province. Frogs and [salamanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salamander "Salamander") are a few of the [amphibians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphibian "Amphibian") that make their homes in Alberta.

Alberta is the only province in Canada — as well as one of the few places in the world — that is free from [Norwegian rats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_rat "Brown rat").[\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-44) Since the early 1950s, the [Government of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Council_of_Alberta "Executive Council of Alberta") has operated a rat-control program, which has been so successful that only isolated instances of wild rat sightings are reported, usually of rats arriving in the province aboard trucks or by rail. In 2006, Alberta Agriculture reported zero findings of wild rats; the only rat interceptions have been domesticated rats that have been seized from their owners. It is illegal for individual Albertans to own or keep Norwegian rats of any description; the animals can only be kept in the province by zoos, universities and colleges, and recognized research institutions. In 2009, several rats were found and captured, in small pockets in southern Alberta,[\[45\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Rodents_defying_Alberta's_rat-free_claim-45) putting Alberta's rat-free status in jeopardy. A colony of rats was subsequently found in a landfill near [Medicine Hat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicine_Hat "Medicine Hat") in 2012 and again in 2014.[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-46)[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-47)

### Paleontology

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=7 "Edit section: Paleontology")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Royal_Tyrrell_Museum_of_Palaeontology.jpg/370px-Royal_Tyrrell_Museum_of_Palaeontology.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Royal_Tyrrell_Museum_of_Palaeontology.jpg)

Specimens at the [Royal Tyrrell Museum of Palaeontology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Tyrrell_Museum_of_Palaeontology "Royal Tyrrell Museum of Palaeontology"), in the [Horseshoe Canyon Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horseshoe_Canyon_Formation "Horseshoe Canyon Formation") at [Dinosaur Provincial Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_Provincial_Park "Dinosaur Provincial Park"). Some of the specimens, from left to right, are *[Hypacrosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypacrosaurus "Hypacrosaurus")*, *[Edmontosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmontosaurus "Edmontosaurus")*, *[Lambeosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lambeosaurus "Lambeosaurus")*, *[Gorgosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorgosaurus "Gorgosaurus")* (both in the background), *[Tyrannosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrannosaurus "Tyrannosaurus")*, and *[Triceratops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triceratops "Triceratops")*.

Alberta has one of the greatest diversities and abundances of [Late Cretaceous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Cretaceous "Late Cretaceous") dinosaur fossils worldwide.[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alberta-paleo-48) [Taxa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxon "Taxon") are represented by complete fossil skeletons, isolated material, microvertebrate remains, and even [mass graves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bone_bed "Bone bed"). At least 38 dinosaur [type specimens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_\(biology\) "Type (biology)") were collected in the province. The [Foremost Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foremost_Formation "Foremost Formation"), [Oldman Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oldman_Formation "Oldman Formation") and [Dinosaur Park Formations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_Park_Formation "Dinosaur Park Formation") collectively comprise the [Judith River Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judith_River_Group "Judith River Group") and are the most thoroughly studied dinosaur-bearing strata in Alberta.[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alberta-paleo-48)

Dinosaur-bearing strata are distributed widely throughout Alberta.[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alberta-paleo-48) The [Dinosaur Provincial Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_Provincial_Park "Dinosaur Provincial Park") area contains outcrops of the Dinosaur Park Formation and Oldman Formation. In Alberta's central and southern regions are intermittent [Scollard Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scollard_Formation "Scollard Formation") outcrops. In the [Drumheller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drumheller "Drumheller") Valley and [Edmonton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmonton "Edmonton") regions there are exposed [Horseshoe Canyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horseshoe_Canyon_Formation "Horseshoe Canyon Formation") [facies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facies "Facies"). Other [formations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geological_formation "Geological formation") have been recorded as well, like the [Milk River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milk_River_Formation "Milk River Formation") and Foremost Formations. The latter two have a lower diversity of documented dinosaurs, primarily due to their lower total fossil quantity and neglect from collectors who are hindered by the isolation and scarcity of exposed outcrops. Their dinosaur fossils are primarily teeth recovered from microvertebrate fossil sites. Additional geologic formations that have produced only a few fossils are the [Belly River Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belly_River_Group "Belly River Group") and [St. Mary River Formations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Mary_River_Formation "St. Mary River Formation") of the southwest and the northwestern [Wapiti Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wapiti_Formation "Wapiti Formation"), which contains two *[Pachyrhinosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pachyrhinosaurus "Pachyrhinosaurus")* bone beds. The [Bearpaw Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bearpaw_Formation "Bearpaw Formation") represents strata deposited during a [marine transgression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_transgression "Marine transgression"). Dinosaurs are known from this formation, but represent specimens washed out to sea or reworked from older [sediments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sediment "Sediment").[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alberta-paleo-48)

## History

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=8 "Edit section: History")]

Main article: [History of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Alberta "History of Alberta")

See also: [Timeline of Alberta history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Alberta_history "Timeline of Alberta history")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/Blackfoot_warriors%2C_Macleod%2C_Alberta_%28HS85-10-18724%29_original.tif/lossy-page1-220px-Blackfoot_warriors%2C_Macleod%2C_Alberta_%28HS85-10-18724%29_original.tif.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Blackfoot_warriors,_Macleod,_Alberta_\(HS85-10-18724\)_original.tif)

[Blackfoot Confederacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackfoot_Confederacy "Blackfoot Confederacy") warriors in [Macleod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Macleod "Fort Macleod") in 1907

[Paleo-Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleo-Indians "Paleo-Indians") arrived in what would later be Alberta at least 10,000 years ago, toward the end of the [last ice age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Glacial_Period "Last Glacial Period"). They are thought to have migrated from [Siberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siberia "Siberia") to [Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska "Alaska") on a [land bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beringia "Beringia") across the [Bering Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bering_Strait "Bering Strait") and then possibly moved south along the east side of the Rocky Mountains through Alberta, settling along the way or moving on to [settle other parts of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Settlement_of_the_Americas "Settlement of the Americas"). Others may have [travelled south along the west coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coastal_migration_\(Americas\) "Coastal migration (Americas)") and then moved inland.[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-49) Over time they differentiated into various [First Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Nations_in_Canada "First Nations in Canada") peoples, including the [Plains Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plains_Indians "Plains Indians") of southern Alberta such as those of the [Blackfoot Confederacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackfoot_Confederacy "Blackfoot Confederacy") and the Plains Cree, who generally lived by [hunting buffalo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bison_hunting "Bison hunting"), and the more northerly tribes such as the [Woodland Cree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodland_Cree "Woodland Cree") and [Chipewyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chipewyan "Chipewyan") who hunted and trapped other types of animals, and fished for a living.[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-aboutab-28)

The first Europeans to visit Alberta were French Canadian fur traders in the early 18th century. The first British subject to visit Alberta was Anthony Henday, in 1754. French Canadians integrated with local First Nations creating the Metis nation, with elements across the Prairies. French was the predominant European language in Alberta and was used in some early fur trading forts in the region, such as the first Fort Edmonton (in present-day Fort Saskatchewan), operated by the [Hudson's Bay Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson%27s_Bay_Company "Hudson's Bay Company") (HBC). After the [British arrival in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_America "British America"), approximately half of Alberta's current territory, south of the [Athabasca River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athabasca_River "Athabasca River") drainage, became part of [Rupert's Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert%27s_Land "Rupert's Land"), which consisted of all land drained by rivers flowing into [Hudson Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_Bay "Hudson Bay"). This area was granted by [King Charles II of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Charles_II_of_England "King Charles II of England") to the Hudson's Bay Company in 1670, and rival fur trading companies were not allowed to trade in it.

The Athabasca River and the rivers north of it were not in HBC territory, because they drained into the [Arctic Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_Ocean "Arctic Ocean") instead of Hudson Bay. The north part of Alberta was a prime habitat for fur-bearing animals and was targeted by the HBC and other [fur trading companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_fur_trade "North American fur trade").

The first European explorer of the Athabasca region was fur trader [Peter Pond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Pond "Peter Pond"), who learned of the [Methye Portage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methye_Portage "Methye Portage"), a convenient route to travel from rivers in the Hudson Bay watershed to rivers north of Rupert's Land. He and other Canadian fur traders formed the [North West Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_West_Company "North West Company") (NWC) of [Montreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montreal "Montreal") in 1779, to compete with the HBC. The NWC built posts at many points across the northern part of Alberta territory. Peter Pond built Fort Athabasca on [Lac la Biche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lac_la_Biche_\(Alberta\) "Lac la Biche (Alberta)") in 1778. [Roderick Mackenzie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roderick_Mackenzie_of_Terrebonne "Roderick Mackenzie of Terrebonne") built [Fort Chipewyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Chipewyan "Fort Chipewyan") on [Lake Athabasca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Athabasca "Lake Athabasca") ten years later in 1788. His cousin, Sir [Alexander Mackenzie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Mackenzie_\(explorer\) "Alexander Mackenzie (explorer)"), followed the [North Saskatchewan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Saskatchewan_River "North Saskatchewan River") to its northernmost point near Edmonton, then trekked on foot to the Athabasca River, which he followed downstream to Lake Athabasca. It was there he discovered the mighty outflow river that bears his name – the [Mackenzie River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mackenzie_River "Mackenzie River") – which he followed to its outlet in the Arctic Ocean. Returning to Lake Athabasca, he followed the [Peace River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_River "Peace River") upstream and crossed the Rockies, eventually reaching the Pacific Ocean, and so he became the first European to cross the North American continent north of Mexico.[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-50)

The extreme southernmost portion of Alberta was part of the French (and Spanish) territory of [Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_\(New_France\) "Louisiana (New France)"), which was [sold to the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase "Louisiana Purchase") in 1803. In the [Treaty of 1818](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_1818 "Treaty of 1818"), the portion of the Louisiana territory north of the [Forty-Ninth Parallel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/49th_parallel_north "49th parallel north") was ceded to the United Kingdom.[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-51) The area was grouped with Rupert's Land to make the [North-Western Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North-Western_Territory "North-Western Territory").

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d3/1820_george_back_fort_chipewyan.jpg/220px-1820_george_back_fort_chipewyan.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:1820_george_back_fort_chipewyan.jpg)

[Fort Chipewyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Chipewyan "Fort Chipewyan"), a trading post and regional headquarters for the [Hudson's Bay Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson%27s_Bay_Company "Hudson's Bay Company") in 1820

Fur trade expanded in the north, but there was intense friction and competition between the rival HBC and NWC. In 1821 the British government forced them to merge to stop the hostilities.[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-52) After amalgamation, the Hudson's Bay Company dominated the economy of Alberta until 1870, when HBC control of Rupert's Land was ended and the territory was transferred to the newly federated Canada. Southern Alberta, Northern Alberta, other parts of the Northland and Rupert's land became Canada's [North-West Territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North-West_Territories "North-West Territories").

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/East_Village_Calgary_Flood_2013.jpg/220px-East_Village_Calgary_Flood_2013.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:East_Village_Calgary_Flood_2013.jpg)

[Downtown Calgary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Calgary "Downtown Calgary") was one of several areas afflicted during the [2013 Alberta floods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Alberta_floods "2013 Alberta floods").

First Nations and representatives of [the Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crown "The Crown") negotiated the [Numbered Treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Numbered_Treaties "Numbered Treaties"), in which the Crown gained title to the land that would later become Alberta, and the Crown committed to the ongoing support of the First Nations and guaranteed their hunting and fishing rights. The most significant treaties for Alberta are [Treaty 6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_6 "Treaty 6") (1876), [Treaty 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_7 "Treaty 7") (1877) and [Treaty 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_8 "Treaty 8") (1899).

The [District of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Alberta "District of Alberta") was created as part of the North-West Territories in 1882. As settlement increased, local representatives to the [North-West Legislative Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North-West_Legislative_Assembly "North-West Legislative Assembly") and the House of Commons were elected, and senators appointed, to represent Alberta. After a long campaign for autonomy, in 1905, the District of Alberta was enlarged and given provincial status, with [the election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1905_Alberta_general_election "1905 Alberta general election") of a [Liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Liberal_Party "Alberta Liberal Party") majority with [Alexander Cameron Rutherford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Cameron_Rutherford "Alexander Cameron Rutherford") as the first premier. At first the economy was very active, then around 1912, Alberta suffered a recession. The [First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_World_War "First World War") presented special challenges to the new province as an extraordinary number of working-age men volunteered for active service, leaving relatively few workers to maintain services and production. Over 50% of Alberta's doctors volunteered for service overseas.[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-53)

In 1918 Albertans experienced the [1918 flu epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_flu "Spanish flu").

Alberta voters sought innovation, electing a [Farmers government in 1921](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1921_Alberta_general_election "1921 Alberta general election"), then the world's first [Social Credit government in 1935](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1935_Alberta_general_election "1935 Alberta general election").

Alberta's economy stayed sluggish, especially during the Depression. But [discovery of oil at Leduc in 1946](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leduc_No._1 "Leduc No. 1") opened a new era of prosperity and wealth for the province.

On June 21, 2013, during the [2013 Alberta floods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Alberta_floods "2013 Alberta floods") Alberta experienced heavy rainfall that triggered catastrophic flooding throughout much of the southern half of the province along the [Bow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bow_River "Bow River"), [Elbow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elbow_River "Elbow River"), [Highwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highwood_River "Highwood River") and [Oldman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oldman_River "Oldman River") rivers and tributaries. A dozen municipalities in Southern Alberta declared local states of emergency on June 21 as water levels rose and numerous communities were placed under evacuation orders.[\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-54)

In 2016, the [Fort McMurray wildfire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Fort_McMurray_wildfire "2016 Fort McMurray wildfire") resulted in the largest fire evacuation of residents in Alberta's history, as more than 80,000 people were ordered to evacuate.[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-55)[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-56)

Like the rest of the world, Alberta was affected by the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Alberta "COVID-19 pandemic in Alberta") starting in 2020. The last restrictions were lifted in 2022.[\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CBC_20201019-57)

## Demographics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=9 "Edit section: Demographics")]

Main article: [Demographics of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Alberta "Demographics of Alberta")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Alberta_population_density_2021.svg/290px-Alberta_population_density_2021.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Alberta_population_density_2021.svg)

Population density of Alberta

The [2021 Canadian census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Canadian_census "2021 Canadian census") reported Alberta had a population of 4,262,635 living in 1,633,220 of its 1,772,670 total dwellings, an 4.8% change from its 2016 population of 4,067,175. With a land area of 634,658.27 km2 (245,042.93 sq mi), it had a population density of 6.7/km2 in 2021.[\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Alberta-2) [Statistics Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statistics_Canada "Statistics Canada") estimated the province to have a population of 4,931,601 in Q4 of 2024.[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-StatCan2017Q1Est-58)

Since 2000, Alberta's population has experienced a relatively high rate of growth, mainly because of its burgeoning economy. Between 2003 and 2004, the province had high birthrates (on par with some larger provinces such as British Columbia), relatively high immigration, and a high rate of [interprovincial migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interprovincial_migration_in_Canada "Interprovincial migration in Canada") compared to other provinces.[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Components_of_population_growth_of_Alberta-59)

In 2016, Alberta continued to have the youngest population among the provinces with a median age of 36.7 years, compared with the national median of 41.2 years. Also in 2016, Alberta had the smallest proportion of seniors (12.3%) among the provinces and one of the highest population shares of children (19.2%), further contributing to Alberta's young and growing population.[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-60)

About 81% of the population lives in urban areas and only about 19% in rural areas. The [Calgary–Edmonton Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calgary%E2%80%93Edmonton_Corridor "Calgary–Edmonton Corridor") is the most urbanized area in the province and is one of the most densely populated areas of Canada.[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-muntypes-61) Many of Alberta's cities and towns have experienced very high rates of growth in recent history.\[*[when?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items "Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers")*] Alberta's population rose from 73,022 in 1901[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Population_urban_and_rural_Alberta-62) to 3,290,350 according to the [2006 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Canadian_census "2006 Canadian census").[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Population_and_dwelling_counts,_for_Alberta-63)

According to the 2016 census Alberta has 779,155 residents (19.2%) between the ages of 0–14, 2,787,805 residents (68.5%) between the ages of 15–64, and 500,215 residents (12.3%) aged 65 and over.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:0-64)

Additionally, as per the 2016 census, 1,769,500 residents hold a postsecondary certificate, diploma or degree, 895,885 residents have obtained a secondary (high) school diploma or equivalency certificate, and 540,665 residents do not have any certificate, diploma or degree.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:0-64)
