### Language

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=11 "Edit section: Language")]

Main article: [Demographics of Alberta § Languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Alberta#Languages "Demographics of Alberta")

As of the [2021 Canadian Census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Canadian_census "2021 Canadian census"), the ten most spoken languages in the province included English (4,109,720 or 98.37%), French (260,415 or 6.23%), Tagalog (172,625 or 4.13%), Punjabi (126,385 or 3.03%), Spanish (116,070 or 2.78%), Hindi (94,015 or 2.25%), Mandarin (82,095 or 1.97%), Arabic (76,760 or 1.84%), Cantonese (74,960 or 1.79%), and German (65,370 or 1.56%).[\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-language2021-75) The question on knowledge of languages allows for multiple responses.

As of the 2016 census, English is the most common mother tongue, with 2,991,485 native speakers.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:0-64) This is followed by [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_language "Tagalog language"), with 99,035 speakers, German, with 80,050 speakers, French, with 72,150 native speakers, and [Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_language "Punjabi language"), with 68,695 speakers.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:0-64)

The 2006 census found that English, with 2,576,670 native speakers, was the most common mother tongue of Albertans, representing 79.99% of the population. The next most common mother tongues were Chinese with 97,275 native speakers (3.02%), followed by German with 84,505 native speakers (2.62%) and French with 61,225 (1.90%).[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Detailed_Mother_Tongue_Alberta-76) Other mother tongues include: [Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_language "Punjabi language"), with 36,320 native speakers (1.13%); [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_language "Tagalog language"), with 29,740 (0.92%); [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_language "Ukrainian language"), with 29,455 (0.91%); Spanish, with 29,125 (0.90%); [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language "Polish language"), with 21,990 (0.68%); [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic "Arabic"), with 20,495 (0.64%); [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language "Dutch language"), with 19,980 (0.62%); and [Vietnamese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamese_language "Vietnamese language"), with 19,350 (0.60%). The most common aboriginal language is [Cree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cree_language "Cree language") 17,215 (0.53%). Other common mother tongues include Italian with 13,095 speakers (0.41%); [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu "Urdu") with 11,275 (0.35%); and [Korean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_language "Korean language") with 10,845 (0.33%); then [Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi "Hindi") 8,985 (0.28%); [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language "Persian language") 7,700 (0.24%); [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language "Portuguese language") 7,205 (0.22%); and [Hungarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_language "Hungarian language") 6,770 (0.21%).

According to Statistics Canada, Alberta is home to the second-highest proportion (2%) of [Francophones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographical_distribution_of_French_speakers "Geographical distribution of French speakers") in western Canada (after [Manitoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manitoba "Manitoba")). Despite this, relatively few Albertans claim French as their mother tongue. Many of [Alberta's French-speaking residents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Albertans "Franco-Albertans") live in the central and northwestern regions of the province, after migration from other areas of Canada or descending from Métis.

### Ethnicity

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=12 "Edit section: Ethnicity")]

Main article: [Demographics of Alberta § Ethnic origins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Alberta#Ethnic_origins "Demographics of Alberta")

Alberta has considerable ethnic diversity. In line with the rest of Canada, many Albertans are descended from immigrants of Western European nations, notably [England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Canadians "English Canadians"), [Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Canadians "Scottish Canadians"), [Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Canadians "Irish Canadians"), [Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_Canadians "Welsh Canadians") and [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Canadians "French Canadians"), but large numbers later came from other regions of Europe, notably [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Canadians "German Canadians"), [Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Canadians "Ukrainian Canadians") and [Scandinavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_and_Scandinavian_Canadians "Nordic and Scandinavian Canadians"). More recently, Africans, Asians and South Americans in larger numbers have also contributed to Alberta's mosaic.[\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-77)

Many Alberta familes today can trace their ethnicity in many directions. In the 2006 [Canadian census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Census_in_Canada "Census in Canada"), the most commonly reported ethnic origins among Albertans were: 885,825 English (27.2%); 679,705 German (20.9%); 667,405 Canadian (20.5%); 661,265 Scottish (20.3%); 539,160 Irish (16.6%); 388,210 French (11.9%); 332,180 Ukrainian (10.2%); 172,910 [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Canadians "Dutch Canadians") (5.3%); 170,935 [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Canadians "Polish Canadians") (5.2%); 169,355 [North American Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas "Indigenous peoples of the Americas") (5.2%); 144,585 [Norwegian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegian_Canadians "Norwegian Canadians") (4.4%); and 137,600 [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Canadians "Chinese Canadians") (4.2%). (Each person could choose as many ethnicities as were applicable so the percentages add up to much more than 100.)[\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Ethnic_origins,_2006_counts,_for_Alberta-78)

Amongst those of British heritage, the Scots have had a particularly strong influence on place-names. Many cities and towns have names of [Scottish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Scottish_place_names_in_Canada "List of Scottish place names in Canada") origins, such as Calgary, [Airdrie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airdrie,_Alberta "Airdrie, Alberta"), [Canmore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canmore,_Alberta "Canmore, Alberta"), and Banff.

Both Edmonton and Calgary have historic [Chinatowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown "Chinatown"), and Calgary has Canada's third-largest Chinese community. The Chinese presence began with workers employed in the building of the [Canadian Pacific Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Pacific_Railway "Canadian Pacific Railway") in the 1880s.

In 2021, 27.8% of the population consisted of [visible minorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visible_minorities "Visible minorities") and 6.8% of the population was [Indigenous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_in_Canada "Indigenous peoples in Canada"), mostly of [First Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Nations_in_Canada "First Nations in Canada") and [Métis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9tis_in_Canada "Métis in Canada") descent. A small number of [Inuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inuit "Inuit") live in the province. The Indigenous population has been growing at a faster rate than the population of Alberta as a whole. Some of this increase is due to Albertans who are only now embracing their Metis lineage.[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Aboriginal_identity_population_by_age_groups_Alberta-79)[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-80)

### Religion

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=13 "Edit section: Religion")]

Main article: [Demographics of Alberta § Religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Alberta#Religion "Demographics of Alberta")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/St_Josaphat_from_W.jpg/220px-St_Josaphat_from_W.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:St_Josaphat_from_W.jpg)

[St. Josaphat Ukrainian Catholic Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Josaphat_Cathedral "St. Josaphat Cathedral") in Edmonton

According to the [2021 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Canadian_census "2021 Canadian census"), religious groups in Alberta included:[\[81\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-2021censusB-81)

- [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Canada "Christianity in Canada") (2,009,820 persons or 48.1%)
- [Irreligion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_Canada "Irreligion in Canada") (1,676,045 persons or 40.1%)
- [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Canada "Islam in Canada") (202,535 persons or 4.8%)
- [Sikhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_Canada "Sikhism in Canada") (103,600 persons or 2.5%)
- [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Canada "Hinduism in Canada") (78,520 persons or 1.9%)
- [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Canada "Buddhism in Canada") (42,830 persons or 1.0%)
- [Indigenous Spirituality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mythologies_of_the_indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas "Mythologies of the indigenous peoples of the Americas") (19,755 persons or 0.5%)
- [Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaism_in_Canada "Judaism in Canada") (11,390 persons or 0.3%)
- Other (33,220 persons or 0.8%)

As of the [2011 National Household Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Canadian_census "2011 Canadian census"), the largest religious group was Roman Catholic, representing 24.3% of the population. Alberta had the second-highest percentage of [non-religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion "Irreligion") residents among the provinces (after British Columbia) at 31.6% of the population. Of the remainder, 7.5% of the population identified themselves as belonging to the [United Church of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Church_of_Canada "United Church of Canada"), while 3.9% were [Anglican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Church_of_Canada "Anglican Church of Canada"). [Lutherans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lutheranism "Lutheranism") made up 3.3% of the population while [Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptists "Baptists") comprised 1.9%.[\[82\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-NHS_Profile,_Alberta,_2011-82)

Members of [LDS Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints "The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints") are mostly concentrated in the extreme south of the province. Alberta has a population of [Hutterites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hutterites "Hutterites"), a communal [Anabaptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anabaptism "Anabaptism") sect similar to the [Mennonites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mennonites "Mennonites"), and has a significant population of [Seventh-day Adventists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventh-day_Adventist_Church "Seventh-day Adventist Church"). Alberta is home to several [Byzantine Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Rite "Byzantine Rite") Churches as part of the legacy of Eastern European immigration, including the [Ukrainian Catholic Eparchy of Edmonton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Catholic_Eparchy_of_Edmonton "Ukrainian Catholic Eparchy of Edmonton"), and the [Ukrainian Orthodox Church of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Orthodox_Church_of_Canada "Ukrainian Orthodox Church of Canada")'s [Western Diocese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Orthodox_Eparchy_of_Western_Canada "Ukrainian Orthodox Eparchy of Western Canada") which is based in Edmonton. Muslims made up 3.2% of the population, Sikhs 1.5%, Buddhists 1.2%, and Hindus 1.0%. Many of these are immigrants, but others have roots that go back to the first settlers of the prairies. Canada's oldest mosque, the [Al-Rashid Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Rashid_Mosque "Al-Rashid Mosque"), is in Edmonton,[\[83\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Al-Rashid_Mosque-83) whereas Calgary is home to Canada's largest mosque, the [Baitun Nur Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baitun_Nur_Mosque "Baitun Nur Mosque").[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Baitun_Nur-84) Alberta is also home to a growing Jewish population of about 15,400 people who constituted 0.3% of Alberta's population. Most of Alberta's Jews live in the metropolitan areas of Calgary (8,200) and Edmonton (5,500).[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-85)

## Economy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=14 "Edit section: Economy")]

Main article: [Economy of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Alberta "Economy of Alberta")

See also: [List of Canadian provinces and territories by gross domestic product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Canadian_provinces_and_territories_by_gross_domestic_product "List of Canadian provinces and territories by gross domestic product")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/Petroleum_resources_in_Alberta%2C_according_to_the_Argonne_National_Labs.jpg/290px-Petroleum_resources_in_Alberta%2C_according_to_the_Argonne_National_Labs.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Petroleum_resources_in_Alberta,_according_to_the_Argonne_National_Labs.jpg)

Petroleum resources in Alberta

Alberta's economy has been one of the strongest in the world, supported by the burgeoning petroleum industry and to a lesser extent, agriculture and technology. In 2013, Alberta's per capita GDP exceeded that of the United States, Norway, or Switzerland,[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-86) and was the highest of any province in Canada at CA$84,390. This was 56% higher than the national average of CA$53,870 and more than twice that of some of the [Atlantic provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Canada "Atlantic Canada").[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-87)[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-88) In 2006, the deviation from the national average was the largest for any province in [Canadian history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Canada "History of Canada").[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-89) According to the 2006 census,[\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Median_earnings_for_economic_families_with_earnings_Alberta-90) the median annual family income after taxes was $70,986 in Alberta (compared to $60,270 in Canada as a whole). In 2014, Alberta had the second-largest economy in Canada after Ontario, with a GDP exceeding CA$376 billion.[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-91) The GDP of the province calculated at basic prices rose by 4.6% in 2017 to $327.4 billion, which was the largest increase recorded in Canada, and it ended two consecutive years of decreases.[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-92)

Alberta's [debt-to-GDP ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debt-to-GDP_ratio "Debt-to-GDP ratio") is projected to peak at 12.1% in [fiscal year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal_year "Fiscal year") 2021–2022, falling to 11.3% the following year.[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-93)

The Calgary-Edmonton Corridor is the most urbanized region in the province and one of the densest in Canada. The region covers a distance of roughly 400 km (250 mi) north to south. In 2001, the population of the Calgary-Edmonton Corridor was 2.15 million (72% of Alberta's population).[\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CECorridor-94) It is also one of the fastest-growing regions in the country by population. A 2003 study by [TD Bank Financial Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toronto-Dominion_Bank "Toronto-Dominion Bank") found the corridor to be the only Canadian urban centre to amass a United States level of wealth while maintaining a Canadian style [quality of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quality_of_life "Quality of life"), offering [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care "Universal health care") benefits. The report found that GDP per capita in the corridor was 10% above average United States metropolitan areas and 40% above other [Canadian cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Canada "List of cities in Canada") at that time.[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-95)

The [Fraser Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fraser_Institute "Fraser Institute") states that Alberta also has very high levels of [economic freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_freedom "Economic freedom") and rates Alberta as the freest economy in Canada,[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-96) and second-freest economy amongst U.S. states and Canadian provinces.[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-97)

In 2014, merchandise exports totalled US$121.4 billion. Energy revenues totalled $111.7 billion and Energy resource exports totalled $90.8 billion. Farm Cash receipts from agricultural products totalled $12.9 billion. Shipments of forest products totalled $5.4 billion while exports were $2.7 billion. Manufacturing sales totalled $79.4 billion, and Alberta's [information and communications technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_and_communications_technology "Information and communications technology") (ICT) industries generated over $13 billion in revenue. In total, Alberta's 2014 GDP amassed $364.5 billion in 2007 dollars, or $414.3 billion in 2015 dollars. In 2015, Alberta's GDP grew unstably despite low oil prices, with growth rates as high 4.4% and as low as 0.2%.[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-albertacanada.com-98)[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-99)

### Agriculture and forestry

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=15 "Edit section: Agriculture and forestry")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Cows_in_the_Foothills_-_panoramio.jpg/220px-Cows_in_the_Foothills_-_panoramio.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cows_in_the_Foothills_-_panoramio.jpg)

Cows in [Rocky View](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_View_County "Rocky View County"). Nearly one-half of Canadian beef is produced here.

Agriculture has a significant position in the province's economy. The province has over three million head of cattle,[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-100) and Alberta beef has a healthy worldwide market. Forty percent of all Canadian beef is produced in Alberta.[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-101) The province also produces the most [bison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_bison "American bison") meat in Canada.[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-102) Sheep for wool and mutton are also raised.[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-103)

Wheat and [canola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canola "Canola")[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Canola-104) are primary farm crops, with Alberta leading the provinces in [spring wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spring_wheat "Spring wheat") production; other [grains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cereal "Cereal") are also prominent. Much of the farming is dryland farming, often with fallow seasons interspersed with cultivation. Continuous cropping (in which there is no fallow season) is gradually becoming a more common mode of production because of increased profits and a reduction of [soil erosion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_erosion "Soil erosion"). Across the province, the once common [grain elevator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grain_elevator "Grain elevator") is slowly being lost as rail lines are decreasing; farmers typically truck the grain to central points.[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-105)

Alberta is the leading [beekeeping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beekeeping "Beekeeping") province of Canada, with some beekeepers wintering [hives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beehive "Beehive") indoors in specially designed barns in southern Alberta, then migrating north during the summer into the [Peace River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_River "Peace River") valley where the season is short but the working days are long for [honeybees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_honey_bee "Western honey bee") to produce honey from [clover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clover "Clover") and [fireweed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamaenerion_angustifolium "Chamaenerion angustifolium"). [Hybrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_\(biology\) "Hybrid (biology)") canola also requires bee pollination, and some beekeepers service this need.[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-106)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Canola_in_Alberta_Canada.jpg/220px-Canola_in_Alberta_Canada.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Canola_in_Alberta_Canada.jpg)

[Canola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canola "Canola") field, [Edmonton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmonton "Edmonton")

Forestry plays a vital role in Alberta's economy, providing over 15,000 jobs and contributing billions of dollars annually.[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-gov.ab.ca-107) Uses for harvested timber include [pulpwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulpwood "Pulpwood"), [hardwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardwood "Hardwood"), [engineered wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Engineered_wood "Engineered wood") and [bioproducts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bioproducts "Bioproducts") such as chemicals and [biofuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biofuel "Biofuel").

### Industry

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=16 "Edit section: Industry")]

Alberta is the largest producer of [conventional crude oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleum "Petroleum"), [synthetic crude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synthetic_crude "Synthetic crude"), [natural gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas "Natural gas") and gas products in Canada. Alberta is the world's second-largest exporter of natural gas and the fourth-largest producer.[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Alaska_and_Alberta_-_An_Overview-108) Two of the largest producers of [petrochemicals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrochemical "Petrochemical") in North America are in central and north-central Alberta. In both Red Deer and Edmonton, [polyethylene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyethylene "Polyethylene") and [vinyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyvinyl_chloride "Polyvinyl chloride") manufacturers produce products that are shipped all over the world. Edmonton's [oil refineries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_refinery "Oil refinery") provide the raw materials for a large [petrochemical industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrochemical_industry "Petrochemical industry") to the east of Edmonton.

The [Athabasca oil sands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athabasca_oil_sands "Athabasca oil sands") surrounding [Fort McMurray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_McMurray "Fort McMurray") have estimated [unconventional oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unconventional_oil "Unconventional oil") reserves approximately equal to the conventional oil reserves of the rest of the world, estimated to be 1.6×1012 barrels (250 km3).[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-a496-109) Many companies employ both conventional [strip mining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surface_mining "Surface mining") and non-conventional [in situ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_situ "In situ") methods to extract the [bitumen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitumen "Bitumen") from the [oil sands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_sands "Oil sands"). As of late 2006, there were over $100 billion in oil sands projects under construction or in the planning stages in northeastern Alberta.[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Canada's_Oil_and_Gas_Industry-110)

Another factor determining the viability of oil extraction from the oil sands is the [price of oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Price_of_oil "Price of oil"). The [oil price increases since 2003](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_oil_market_chronology_from_2003 "World oil market chronology from 2003") have made it profitable to extract this oil, which in the past would give little profit or even a loss. By mid-2014, rising costs and stabilizing oil prices threatened the economic viability of some projects. An example of this was the shelving of the Joslyn North project[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-z848-111) in the Athabasca region in May 2014.[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-TotalJoslyn-112)

With concerted effort and support from the provincial government, several high-tech industries have found their birth in Alberta, notably patents related to interactive [liquid-crystal display](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid-crystal_display "Liquid-crystal display") systems.[\[113\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-113) With a growing economy, Alberta has several financial institutions dealing with civil and private funds.

### Tourism

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=17 "Edit section: Tourism")]

Main article: [Tourism in Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Alberta "Tourism in Alberta")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/Three_Sisters_-_panoramio_%287%29.jpg/220px-Three_Sisters_-_panoramio_%287%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Three_Sisters_-_panoramio_\(7\).jpg)

[The Three Sisters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Three_Sisters_\(Alberta\) "The Three Sisters (Alberta)") at [Bow Valley Provincial Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bow_Valley_Provincial_Park "Bow Valley Provincial Park") in [Canmore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canmore,_Alberta "Canmore, Alberta")

Alberta has been a tourist destination from the early days of the 20th century, with attractions including outdoor locales for skiing, hiking, and camping, shopping locales such as [West Edmonton Mall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Edmonton_Mall "West Edmonton Mall"), [Calgary Stampede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calgary_Stampede "Calgary Stampede"), outdoor festivals, professional athletic events, international sporting competitions such as the [Commonwealth Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Games "Commonwealth Games") and Olympic Games, as well as more eclectic attractions. According to Alberta Economic Development, Calgary and Edmonton both host over four million visitors annually. [Banff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banff,_Alberta "Banff, Alberta"), [Jasper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jasper,_Alberta "Jasper, Alberta") and the [Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Rockies "Canadian Rockies") are visited by about three million people per year.[\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Geography_&_Climate:Encourage_Canadian_Immigration-114) Alberta tourism relies heavily on [Southern Ontario](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Ontario "Southern Ontario") tourists, as well as tourists from other parts of Canada, the United States, and many other countries.

There are also natural attractions like [Elk Island National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elk_Island_National_Park "Elk Island National Park"), [Wood Buffalo National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wood_Buffalo_National_Park "Wood Buffalo National Park"), and the [Columbia Icefield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_Icefield "Columbia Icefield"). [Alberta's Rockies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta%27s_Rockies "Alberta's Rockies") include well-known tourist destinations [Banff National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banff_National_Park "Banff National Park") and [Jasper National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jasper_National_Park "Jasper National Park"). The two mountain parks are connected by the scenic [Icefields Parkway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icefields_Parkway "Icefields Parkway"). Banff is located 128 km (80 mi) west of Calgary on [Highway 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Highway_1 "Alberta Highway 1"), and Jasper is located 366 km (227 mi) west of Edmonton on the [Yellowhead Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellowhead_Highway "Yellowhead Highway"). Five of Canada's fourteen [UNESCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO "UNESCO") [World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Site "World Heritage Site") are located within the province: [Canadian Rocky Mountain Parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Rocky_Mountain_Parks_World_Heritage_Site "Canadian Rocky Mountain Parks World Heritage Site"), [Waterton-Glacier International Peace Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waterton-Glacier_International_Peace_Park "Waterton-Glacier International Peace Park"), Wood Buffalo National Park, [Dinosaur Provincial Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_Provincial_Park "Dinosaur Provincial Park") and [Head-Smashed-In Buffalo Jump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head-Smashed-In_Buffalo_Jump "Head-Smashed-In Buffalo Jump"). A number of these areas hold ski resorts, most notably [Banff Sunshine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banff_Sunshine "Banff Sunshine"), [Lake Louise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Louise_Ski_Resort "Lake Louise Ski Resort"), [Marmot Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marmot_Basin "Marmot Basin"), [Norquay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mt_Norquay "Mt Norquay") and [Nakiska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nakiska "Nakiska").

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Bronco_stampede01c.jpg/220px-Bronco_stampede01c.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bronco_stampede01c.jpg)

Bronco riding at the [Calgary Stampede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calgary_Stampede "Calgary Stampede"). The event is one of the world's largest [rodeos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodeo "Rodeo").

About 1.2 million people visit the Calgary Stampede,[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Landmark_Stampede_Dates-115) a celebration of Canada's own Wild West and the cattle ranching industry. About 700,000 people enjoy Edmonton's [K-Days](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K-Days "K-Days") (formerly Klondike Days and Capital EX).[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-KDays-116)[\[117\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-117) Edmonton was the gateway to the only all-Canadian route to the [Yukon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yukon "Yukon") [gold fields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_mining "Gold mining"), and the only route which did not require gold-seekers to travel the exhausting and dangerous [Chilkoot Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chilkoot_Pass "Chilkoot Pass").

Another tourist destination that draws more than 650,000 visitors each year is the Drumheller Valley, located northeast of Calgary. [Drumheller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drumheller "Drumheller"), known as the "Dinosaur Capital of The World", offers the [Royal Tyrrell Museum of Palaeontology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Tyrrell_Museum_of_Palaeontology "Royal Tyrrell Museum of Palaeontology"). Drumheller also had a rich mining history being one of Western Canada's largest coal producers during the war years. Another attraction in east-central Alberta is [Alberta Prairie Railway Excursions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Prairie_Railway_Excursions "Alberta Prairie Railway Excursions"), a popular tourist attraction operated out of [Stettler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stettler,_Alberta "Stettler, Alberta"), that offers train excursions into the prairie and caters to tens of thousands of visitors every year.

## Government and politics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=18 "Edit section: Government and politics")]

Main articles: [Politics of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Alberta "Politics of Alberta") and [Monarchy in Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_in_Alberta "Monarchy in Alberta")

See also: [List of premiers of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_premiers_of_Alberta "List of premiers of Alberta"), [List of Alberta general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Alberta_general_elections "List of Alberta general elections"), [Executive Council of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Council_of_Alberta "Executive Council of Alberta"), and [List of Alberta public agencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Alberta_public_agencies "List of Alberta public agencies")

[![Locations of Alberta's specialized and rural municipalities](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/Alberta%27s_Specialized_and_Rural_Municipalities.png/290px-Alberta%27s_Specialized_and_Rural_Municipalities.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Alberta%27s_Specialized_and_Rural_Municipalities.png)

Distribution of Alberta's [6 specialized municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_specialized_municipalities_in_Alberta "List of specialized municipalities in Alberta") (red) and 74 rural municipalities, which include [municipal districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipal_districts_in_Alberta "List of municipal districts in Alberta") (often named as [counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipal_districts_in_Alberta "List of municipal districts in Alberta")) (orange), [improvement districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Improvement_districts_of_Alberta "Improvement districts of Alberta") (dark green) and [special areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Areas_Board "Special Areas Board") (light green) (2020)

The Government of Alberta is organized as a [parliamentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system "Parliamentary system") democracy with a unicameral legislature. Its [unicameral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unicameralism "Unicameralism") legislature—the [Legislative Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_Assembly_of_Alberta "Legislative Assembly of Alberta")—consists of 87 members elected [first past the post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First-past-the-post_voting "First-past-the-post voting") (FPTP) from single-member constituencies.[\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-118) Locally municipal governments and school boards are elected and operate separately. Their boundaries do not necessarily coincide.

As [King of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_Canada "Monarchy of Canada"), [Charles III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_III "Charles III") is the head of state of Alberta. His duties concerning the Government of Alberta are carried out by [Lieutenant Governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_Governor_of_Alberta "Lieutenant Governor of Alberta") [Salma Lakhani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salma_Lakhani "Salma Lakhani").[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-PM-pressrelease-119) The King and lieutenant governor are figureheads whose actions are highly restricted by custom and [constitutional convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_convention_\(political_custom\) "Constitutional convention (political custom)"). The lieutenant governor handles numerous honorific duties in the name of the King. The government is headed by the [premier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premier_of_Alberta "Premier of Alberta"). The premier is normally a member of the Legislative Assembly, and draws all the members of the Cabinet from among the members of the Legislative Assembly. The City of Edmonton is the seat of the provincial government—the capital of Alberta. The current premier is [Danielle Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danielle_Smith "Danielle Smith"), who was sworn in on October 11, 2022.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Alberta_Legislature_Building.jpg/220px-Alberta_Legislature_Building.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Alberta_Legislature_Building.jpg)

The [Alberta Legislative Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Legislative_Building "Alberta Legislative Building") is the meeting place for the [Legislative Assembly of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_Assembly_of_Alberta "Legislative Assembly of Alberta")

Alberta's elections have tended to yield much more conservative outcomes than those of other Canadian provinces. From the 1980s to the 2010s, Alberta had three main political parties, the [Progressive Conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Association_of_Alberta "Progressive Conservative Association of Alberta") ("Conservatives" or "Tories"), the [Liberals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Liberal_Party "Alberta Liberal Party"), and the social democratic [New Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_New_Democratic_Party "Alberta New Democratic Party"). The [Wildrose Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildrose_Party "Wildrose Party"), a more libertarian party formed in early 2008, gained much support in the [2012 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Alberta_general_election "2012 Alberta general election") and became the [official opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_opposition "Parliamentary opposition"), a role it held until 2017 when it was dissolved and succeeded by the new [United Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Conservative_Party "United Conservative Party") created by the merger of Wildrose and the Progressive Conservatives. The strongly conservative [Social Credit Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Social_Credit_Party "Alberta Social Credit Party") was a power in Alberta for many decades, but fell from the political map after the Progressive Conservatives came to power in 1971.

For 44 years the Progressive Conservatives governed Alberta. They lost the [2015 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Alberta_general_election "2015 Alberta general election") to the NDP (which formed their own government for the first time in provincial history, breaking almost 80 consecutive years of right-wing rule),[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-120) suggesting at the time a possible shift to the left in the province, also indicated by the election of progressive mayors in both of Alberta's major cities.[\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-121) Since becoming a province in 1905, Alberta has seen only five changes of government—only six parties have governed Alberta: the Liberals, from 1905 to 1921; the [United Farmers of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Farmers_of_Alberta "United Farmers of Alberta"), from 1921 to 1935; the Social Credit Party, from 1935 to 1971; the Progressive Conservative Party, from 1971 to 2015; from 2015 to 2019, the Alberta New Democratic Party; and from 2019, the United Conservative Party, with the [most recent transfer of power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Alberta_general_election "2019 Alberta general election") being the first time in provincial history that an incumbent government was not returned to a second term.

### Administrative divisions

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=19 "Edit section: Administrative divisions")]

The province is divided into ten types of [local governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_Canada "Local government in Canada") – urban municipalities (including [cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Alberta "List of cities in Alberta"), [towns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_towns_in_Alberta "List of towns in Alberta"), [villages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_villages_in_Alberta "List of villages in Alberta") and [summer villages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_summer_villages_in_Alberta "List of summer villages in Alberta")), [specialized municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_specialized_municipalities_in_Alberta "List of specialized municipalities in Alberta"), [rural municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_rural_municipalities_in_Alberta "List of rural municipalities in Alberta") (including [municipal districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipal_districts_in_Alberta "List of municipal districts in Alberta") (often named as counties), [improvement districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Improvement_districts_of_Alberta "Improvement districts of Alberta"), and [special areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_communities_in_Alberta#Special_areas "List of communities in Alberta")), [Métis settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9tis_in_Alberta "Métis in Alberta"), and [Indian reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indian_reserves_in_Alberta "List of Indian reserves in Alberta"). All types of municipalities are governed by local residents and were incorporated under various provincial acts, with the exception of improvement districts (governed by either the provincial or federal government), and [Indian reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_reserve "Indian reserve") (governed by local [band governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Band_government "Band government") under federal jurisdiction).

### Law enforcement

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=20 "Edit section: Law enforcement")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/RCMP_officers_Beth%2C_Janice_%26_Harp_2.jpg/220px-RCMP_officers_Beth%2C_Janice_%26_Harp_2.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:RCMP_officers_Beth,_Janice_%26_Harp_2.jpg)

[Royal Canadian Mounted Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Mounted_Police "Royal Canadian Mounted Police") officers in [St. Albert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Albert,_Alberta "St. Albert, Alberta"). The RCMP provides municipal policing throughout most of Alberta.

Policing in the province of Alberta upon its creation was the responsibility of the [Royal Northwest Mounted Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North-West_Mounted_Police "North-West Mounted Police"). In 1917, due to pressures of the First World War, the [Alberta Provincial Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Provincial_Police "Alberta Provincial Police") was created. This organization policed the province until it was disbanded as a [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression "Great Depression")-era cost-cutting measure in 1932. It was at that time the, now renamed, [Royal Canadian Mounted Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Mounted_Police "Royal Canadian Mounted Police") resumed policing of the province, specifically RCMP "K" Division. With the advent of the [Alberta Sheriffs Branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Sheriffs_Branch "Alberta Sheriffs Branch"), the distribution of duties of law enforcement in Alberta has been evolving as certain aspects, such as traffic enforcement, mobile surveillance and the close protection of the Premier of Alberta have been transferred to the Sheriffs. In 2006, Alberta formed the [Alberta Law Enforcement Response Teams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Law_Enforcement_Response_Teams "Alberta Law Enforcement Response Teams") (ALERT) to combat organized crime and the serious offences that accompany it. ALERT is made up of members of the RCMP, Sheriffs Branch, and various major municipal police forces in Alberta.

### Military

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=21 "Edit section: Military")]

Military bases in Alberta include [Canadian Forces Base (CFB) Cold Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CFB_Cold_Lake "CFB Cold Lake"), [CFB Edmonton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CFB_Edmonton "CFB Edmonton"), [CFB Suffield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CFB_Suffield "CFB Suffield") and [CFB Wainwright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CFB_Wainwright "CFB Wainwright"). Air force units stationed at CFB Cold Lake have access to the [Cold Lake Air Weapons Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CFB_Cold_Lake "CFB Cold Lake").[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CFBCL-122) CFB Edmonton is the headquarters for the 3rd Canadian Division.[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CFBEdm-123) CFB Suffield hosts British troops and is the largest training facility in Canada.[\[124\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CFBSuf-124)

### Taxation

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=22 "Edit section: Taxation")]

According to Alberta's 2009 budget, government revenue in that year came mainly from royalties on non-renewable natural resources (30.4%), personal income taxes (22.3%), corporate and other taxes (19.6%), and grants from the [federal government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Canada "Government of Canada") primarily for infrastructure projects (9.8%).[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-budget2009-125) In 2014, Alberta received $6.1 billion in bitumen royalties. With the drop in the price of oil in 2015 it was down to $1.4 billion. In 2016, Alberta received "about $837 million in royalty payments from oil sands Royalty Projects".[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-AB_royalties-126) According to the 2018–2021 fiscal plan, the two top sources of revenue in 2016 were personal income tax at $10,763 million and federal transfers of $7,976 million with total resource revenue at $3,097 million.[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-AB_FiscalPlan_20180322-127): 45  Alberta is the only province in Canada without a provincial [sales tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sales_tax "Sales tax"). Alberta residents are subject to the federal sales tax, the [Goods and Services Tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goods_and_services_tax_\(Canada\) "Goods and services tax (Canada)") of 5%.

|                                      |                                                                                                      |
| ------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Revenue source                       | in millions of dollars[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-AB_FiscalPlan_20180322-127) |
| personal income tax                  | 10,763                                                                                               |
| federal transfers                    | 7,976                                                                                                |
| Other tax revenue                    | 5,649                                                                                                |
| Corporate income tax                 | 3,769                                                                                                |
| Premiums, fees and licenses          | 3,701                                                                                                |
| Investment income                    | 3,698                                                                                                |
| Resource revenue – other             | 1,614                                                                                                |
| Resource revenue – Bitumen royalties | 1,483                                                                                                |
| Net income from business enterprises | 543                                                                                                  |
| Total revenue                        | 42,293                                                                                               |

From 2001 to 2016, Alberta was the only Canadian province to have a [flat tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flat_tax "Flat tax") of 10% of taxable income, which was introduced by Premier, [Ralph Klein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ralph_Klein "Ralph Klein"), as part of the Alberta Tax Advantage, which also included a zero-percent tax on income below a "generous personal exemption".[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Maclean's_Teds_20180509-128)[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CRA_2009-129)

In 2016, under Premier [Rachel Notley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel_Notley "Rachel Notley"), while most Albertans continued to pay the 10% income tax rate, new tax brackets 12%, 14%, and 15% for those with higher incomes ($128,145 annually or more) were introduced.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CBC_Fletcher_20180524-130)[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Maclean's_Teds_20180509-128) Alberta's personal income tax system maintained a [progressive character](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_tax "Progressive tax") by continuing to grant residents personal tax exemptions of $18,451,[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CRA_2015-131) in addition to a variety of tax deductions for persons with disabilities, students, and the aged.[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Alberta_Tax_and_Credits-132) Alberta's municipalities and school jurisdictions have their own governments who usually work in co-operation with the provincial government. By 2018, most Albertans continued to pay the 10% income tax rate.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CBC_Fletcher_20180524-130)

According to a March 2015 [Statistics Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statistics_Canada "Statistics Canada") report, the median household income in Alberta in 2014 was about $100,000, which is 23% higher than the Canadian national average.[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CBC_Johnson_2015-133)

Based on Statistic Canada reports, low-income Albertans, who earn less than $25,000 and those in the high-income bracket earning $150,000 or more, are the lowest-taxed people in Canada.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CBC_Fletcher_20180524-130) Those in the middle income brackets representing those that earn about $25,000 to $75,000[\[Notes 1\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-134) pay more in provincial taxes than residents in British Columbia and Ontario.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CBC_Fletcher_20180524-130) In terms of income tax, Alberta is the "best province" for those with a low income because there is no provincial income tax for those who earn $18,915 or less.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CBC_Fletcher_20180524-130) Even with the 2016 progressive tax brackets up to 15%, Albertans who have the highest incomes, those with a $150,000 annual income or more—about 178,000 people in 2015, pay the least in taxes in Canada.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CBC_Fletcher_20180524-130) — About 1.9 million Albertans earned between $25,000 and $150,000 in 2015.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CBC_Fletcher_20180524-130)

Alberta also privatized alcohol distribution. By 2010, privatization had increased outlets from 304 stores to 1,726; 1,300 jobs to 4,000 jobs; and 3,325 products to 16,495 products.[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-The_Right_Way_to_Sell_Booze_in_New_Brunswick-135) Tax revenue also increased from $400 million to $700 million.

In 2017/18 Alberta collected about $2.4 billion in education property taxes from municipalities.[\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-MA_2018-136) Alberta municipalities raise a significant portion of their income through levying property taxes.[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-qp.alberta-137) The value of assessed property in Alberta was approximately $727 billion in 2011.[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-MA_20110118-138) Most real property is assessed according to its market value.[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-qp.alberta-137) The exceptions to market value assessment are farmland, railways, machinery and equipment and linear property, all of which is assessed by regulated rates.[\[138\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-139) Depending on the property type, property owners may appeal a property assessment to their municipal 'Local Assessment Review Board', 'Composite Assessment Review Board,' or the Alberta Municipal Government Board.[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-qp.alberta-137)[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-140)

## Culture

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=23 "Edit section: Culture")]

Main article: [Culture of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Alberta "Culture of Alberta")

See also: [List of festivals in Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_festivals_in_Alberta "List of festivals in Alberta")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/46/Brazier_Dancers_01.jpg/220px-Brazier_Dancers_01.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Brazier_Dancers_01.jpg)

[Highland dancers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_dancers "Highland dancers") performing at the [CSIO Spruce Meadows 'Masters' Tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CSIO_Spruce_Meadows_%27Masters%27_Tournament "CSIO Spruce Meadows 'Masters' Tournament")

Calgary is famous for [its Stampede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calgary_Stampede "Calgary Stampede"), dubbed "The Greatest Outdoor Show on Earth". The Stampede is Canada's biggest rodeo festival and features various races and competitions, such as [calf roping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calf_roping "Calf roping") and [bull riding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bull_riding "Bull riding"). In line with the western tradition of rodeo are the cultural artisans that reside and create unique Alberta western heritage crafts.

Summer brings many [festivals to Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_festivals_in_Alberta "List of festivals in Alberta"), [especially in Edmonton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_festivals_in_Edmonton "List of festivals in Edmonton"). The [Edmonton Fringe Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmonton_International_Fringe_Festival "Edmonton International Fringe Festival") is the world's second-largest after the [Edinburgh Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edinburgh_Festival_Fringe "Edinburgh Festival Fringe"). Both Calgary and Edmonton host many annual festivals and events, including folk music festivals. The city's "heritage days" festival sees the participation of over 70 ethnic groups. Edmonton's [Churchill Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Churchill_Square_\(Edmonton\) "Churchill Square (Edmonton)") is home to a large number of the festivals, including [A Taste of Edmonton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Taste_of_Edmonton "A Taste of Edmonton") and [The Works Art & Design Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Works_Art_%26_Design_Festival "The Works Art & Design Festival") throughout the summer months.

In 2019, Minister of Culture and Tourism [Ricardo Miranda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Miranda "Ricardo Miranda") announced the Alberta Artist in Residence program in conjunction with the province's first Month of the Artist[\[140\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-141) to celebrate the arts and the value they bring to the province, both socially and economically,[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-142) The artist is selected each year via a public and competitive process is expected to do community outreach and attend events to promote the arts throughout the province. The award comes with $60,000 funding which includes travel and materials costs.[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ArtistInResidence-143) On January 31, 2019, [Lauren Crazybull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lauren_Crazybull "Lauren Crazybull") was named Alberta's first artist in residence.[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-144)[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-145)[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ArtistInResidence-143) Alberta is the first province to launch an artist in residence program in Canada.

## Education

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=25 "Edit section: Education")]

Main article: [Education in Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Alberta "Education in Alberta")

As with any Canadian province, the Alberta Legislature has (almost) exclusive authority to make laws respecting education. Since 1905, the Legislature has used this capacity to continue the model of locally elected public and separate school boards which originated prior to 1905, as well as to create and regulate universities, colleges, technical institutions, and other educational forms and institutions (public charter schools, private schools, homeschooling).

### Elementary and secondary

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=26 "Edit section: Elementary and secondary")]

There are forty-two public school jurisdictions in Alberta, and seventeen operating separate school jurisdictions. Sixteen of the operating separate school jurisdictions have a Catholic electorate, and one ([St. Albert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Albert,_Alberta "St. Albert, Alberta")) has a Protestant electorate. In addition, one Protestant separate school district, Glen Avon, survives as a ward of the St. Paul Education Region. The City of Lloydminster straddles the Albertan/Saskatchewan border, and both the public and separate school systems in that city are counted in the above numbers: both of them operate according to Saskatchewan law.

For many years, the provincial government has funded the greater part of the cost of providing K–12 education. Prior to 1994, public and separate school boards in Alberta had the legislative authority to levy a local tax on property as supplementary support for local education. In 1994, the government of the province eliminated this right for public school boards, but not for separate school boards. Since 1994, there has continued to be a tax on property in support of K–12 education; the difference is that the provincial government now sets the mill rate, the money is collected by the local municipal authority and remitted to the provincial government. The relevant legislation requires that all the money raised by this property tax must go to support K–12 education provided by school boards. The provincial government pools the property tax funds from across the province and distributes them, according to a formula, to public and separate school jurisdictions and Francophone authorities.

[Public](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_school "State school") and separate school boards, charter schools, and private schools all follow the Program of Studies and the curriculum approved by the provincial department of education (Alberta Education). [Homeschool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeschooling "Homeschooling") tutors may choose to follow the Program of Studies or develop their own Program of Studies. Public and separate schools, charter schools, and approved private schools all employ teachers who are certificated by Alberta Education, they administer Provincial Achievement Tests and Diploma Examinations set by Alberta Education, and they may grant high school graduation certificates endorsed by Alberta Education.

### Post-secondary

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=27 "Edit section: Post-secondary")]

Main article: [Higher education in Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_education_in_Alberta "Higher education in Alberta")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/U_of_A.jpg/220px-U_of_A.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:U_of_A.jpg)

The [University of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alberta "University of Alberta") in 2005. The institution is the oldest, and largest university in Alberta.

Several publicly funded post-secondary institutions are governed under the province's *Post-secondary Learning Act*. This includes four comprehensive [research universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_universities "Research universities") that provides undergraduate and graduate degrees, [Athabasca University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athabasca_University "Athabasca University"), the [University of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alberta "University of Alberta"), the [University of Calgary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Calgary "University of Calgary"), and the [University of Lethbridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Lethbridge "University of Lethbridge"); and three undergraduate universities that primarily provide [bachelor's degrees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachelor%27s_degree "Bachelor's degree"), the [Alberta University of the Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_University_of_the_Arts "Alberta University of the Arts"), [Grant MacEwan University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grant_MacEwan_University "Grant MacEwan University"), and [Mount Royal University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Royal_University "Mount Royal University").[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-postsec-146)

Nine comprehensive community colleges offer primarily offer diploma and certificate programs, [Bow Valley College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bow_Valley_College "Bow Valley College"), [Keyano College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keyano_College "Keyano College"), [Lakeland College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakeland_College_\(Alberta\) "Lakeland College (Alberta)"), [Lethbridge College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lethbridge_College "Lethbridge College"), [Medicine Hat College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicine_Hat_College "Medicine Hat College"), [NorQuest College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NorQuest_College "NorQuest College"), [Northern Lakes College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Lakes_College "Northern Lakes College"), [Olds College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olds_College "Olds College"), and [Portage College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portage_College "Portage College"). In addition, there are also four [polytechnic institutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polytechnic_institute "Polytechnic institute") that provide specific career training and provides apprenticeships and diplomas, the [Northern Alberta Institute of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Alberta_Institute_of_Technology "Northern Alberta Institute of Technology"), the [Southern Alberta Institute of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Alberta_Institute_of_Technology "Southern Alberta Institute of Technology"), [Northwestern Polytechnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_Polytechnic "Northwestern Polytechnic"), and [Red Deer Polytechnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Deer_Polytechnic "Red Deer Polytechnic"). The [Banff Centre for Arts and Creativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banff_Centre_for_Arts_and_Creativity "Banff Centre for Arts and Creativity") is a specialized arts and cultural institution that is also empowered to provide diploma programs under the *Post-secondary Learning Act*.[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-postsec-146)

Alberta is also home to five [independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_school "Independent school") postsecondary institutions that provide diplomas/degrees for approved programming, [Ambrose University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambrose_University "Ambrose University"), [Burman University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burman_University "Burman University"), [Concordia University of Edmonton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concordia_University_of_Edmonton "Concordia University of Edmonton"), [The King's University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_King%27s_University_\(Edmonton\) "The King's University (Edmonton)"), and [St. Mary's University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Mary%27s_University_\(Calgary\) "St. Mary's University (Calgary)"). Although the five institutions operate under their own legislation, they remain partly governed by the province's *Post-secondary Learning Act*.[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-postsec-146) In addition to these institutions, there are also 190 private [career colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocational_school "Vocational school") in Alberta.[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-147)

There was some controversy in 2005 over the rising cost of post-secondary education for students (as opposed to taxpayers). In 2005, Premier [Ralph Klein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ralph_Klein "Ralph Klein") made a promise that he would freeze tuition and look into ways of reducing schooling costs.[\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-148)[\[148\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-149)\[*[needs update](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items "Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers")*]

## Health care

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=28 "Edit section: Health care")]

Main article: [Alberta Health Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Health_Services "Alberta Health Services")

See also: [Healthcare in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Canada "Healthcare in Canada")

Alberta provides a [publicly funded, fully integrated health system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Publicly_funded_health_care "Publicly funded health care"), through [Alberta Health Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Health_Services "Alberta Health Services") (AHS)—a quasi-independent agency that delivers health care on behalf of the [Government of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Alberta "Government of Alberta")'s [Ministry of Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Health_\(Alberta\) "Ministry of Health (Alberta)").[\[149\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-AB_GOV_2014-150) The Alberta government provides health services for all its residents as set out by the provisions of the *[Canada Health Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_Health_Act "Canada Health Act")* of 1984. Alberta became Canada's second province (after [Saskatchewan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saskatchewan "Saskatchewan")) to adopt a [Tommy Douglas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tommy_Douglas "Tommy Douglas")-style program in 1950, a precursor to the modern [medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_\(Canada\) "Medicare (Canada)") system.

Alberta's health care budget was $22.5 billion during the 2018–2019 fiscal year (approximately 45% of all government spending), making it the best-funded health-care system per-capita in Canada.[\[150\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-151) Every hour the province spends more than $2.5 million, (or $60 million per day), to maintain and improve health care in the province.[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Alberta_Health_Funding:_Budget_2018-152)

Notable health, education, research, and resources facilities in Alberta, all of which are located within Calgary or Edmonton. Health centres in Calgary include:

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Foothills_Hospital.JPG/220px-Foothills_Hospital.JPG)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Foothills_Hospital.JPG)

[Foothills Medical Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foothills_Medical_Centre "Foothills Medical Centre") in Calgary is the largest hospital in Alberta.

- [Alberta Children's Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Children%27s_Hospital "Alberta Children's Hospital")
- [Foothills Medical Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foothills_Medical_Centre "Foothills Medical Centre")
- Grace Women's Health Centre
- [Libin Cardiovascular Institute of Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libin_Cardiovascular_Institute_of_Alberta "Libin Cardiovascular Institute of Alberta")
- [Peter Lougheed Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Lougheed_Centre "Peter Lougheed Centre")
- [Rockyview General Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockyview_General_Hospital "Rockyview General Hospital")
- [South Health Campus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Health_Campus "South Health Campus")
- [Tom Baker Cancer Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Baker_Cancer_Centre "Tom Baker Cancer Centre")
- University of Calgary Medical Centre (UCMC)

Health centres in Edmonton include:

- Alberta Diabetes Institute
- [Cross Cancer Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross_Cancer_Institute "Cross Cancer Institute")
- Edmonton Clinic
- [Grey Nuns Community Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grey_Nuns_Community_Hospital "Grey Nuns Community Hospital")
- Lois Hole Hospital for Women
- Mazankowski Alberta Heart Institute
- [Misericordia Community Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misericordia_Community_Hospital "Misericordia Community Hospital")
- Rexall Centre for Pharmacy and Health Research
- [Royal Alexandra Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Alexandra_Hospital_\(Edmonton\) "Royal Alexandra Hospital (Edmonton)")
- [Stollery Children's Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stollery_Children%27s_Hospital "Stollery Children's Hospital")
- [University of Alberta Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alberta_Hospital "University of Alberta Hospital")

The [Edmonton Clinic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alberta_in_Edmonton "University of Alberta in Edmonton") complex, completed in 2012, provides a similar research, education, and care environment as the [Mayo Clinic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayo_Clinic "Mayo Clinic") in the United States.[\[152\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Edmonton_Clinic-153)[\[153\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-154)

All public health care services funded by the Government of Alberta are delivered operationally by Alberta Health Services. AHS is the province's single health authority, established on July 1, 2008, which replaced nine regional health authorities. AHS also funds all ground ambulance services in the province, as well as the province-wide [Shock Trauma Air Rescue Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shock_Trauma_Air_Rescue_Service "Shock Trauma Air Rescue Service") (STARS) air ambulance service.[\[154\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-STARS;_About_Us-155)

## Transportation

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=29 "Edit section: Transportation")]

### Air

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=30 "Edit section: Air")]

See also: [List of airports in Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Alberta "List of airports in Alberta")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Calgary_Airport_overview.jpg/220px-Calgary_Airport_overview.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Calgary_Airport_overview.jpg)

[Calgary International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calgary_International_Airport "Calgary International Airport"), the province's largest airport by passenger traffic.

Alberta is well-connected by air, with [international airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_airport "International airport") in both Calgary and Edmonton. [Calgary International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calgary_International_Airport "Calgary International Airport") and [Edmonton International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmonton_International_Airport "Edmonton International Airport") are the fourth- and [fifth-busiest in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_busiest_airports_in_Canada "List of the busiest airports in Canada"), respectively. Calgary's airport is a hub for [WestJet Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WestJet "WestJet") and a regional hub for [Air Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Canada "Air Canada"), primarily serving the prairie provinces (Alberta, Saskatchewan and Manitoba) for connecting flights to British Columbia, eastern Canada, fifteen major United States centres, nine European airports, one Asian airport and four destinations in Mexico and the [Caribbean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caribbean "Caribbean").[\[155\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Calgary_Airport_Authority-156) Edmonton's airport acts as a hub for the Canadian north and has connections to all major Canadian airports as well as airports in the United States, Europe, Mexico, and the Caribbean .[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-EIA-157)

### Public transit

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=31 "Edit section: Public transit")]

Calgary, Edmonton, Red Deer, Medicine Hat, and Lethbridge have substantial [public transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transport "Public transport") systems. In addition to buses, Calgary and Edmonton operate [light rail transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail "Light rail") (LRT) systems. [Edmonton LRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmonton_LRT "Edmonton LRT"), which is underground in the downtown core and on the surface outside the downtown core was the first of the modern generation of light rail systems to be built in North America, while the Calgary [CTrain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CTrain "CTrain") has one of the highest numbers of daily riders of any LRT system in North America.

### Rail

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=32 "Edit section: Rail")]

| Alberta passenger rail                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
