| This diagram:* [view](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template:Alberta_rail_network "Template:Alberta rail network")
* [talk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk:Alberta_rail_network "Template talk:Alberta rail network")
* [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:EditPage/Template:Alberta_rail_network "Special:EditPage/Template:Alberta rail network") |

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/Hawk_Mountain_seen_from_Jasper%2C_Alberta.jpg/220px-Hawk_Mountain_seen_from_Jasper%2C_Alberta.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Hawk_Mountain_seen_from_Jasper,_Alberta.jpg)

A [Via Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Via_Rail "Via Rail") passenger train passing by freight trains in the background, at [Jasper station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jasper_station "Jasper station")

There are more than 9,000 km (5,600 mi) of operating mainline railway in Alberta. The vast majority of this trackage is owned by the [Canadian Pacific Kansas City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Pacific_Kansas_City "Canadian Pacific Kansas City") (CPKC) and [Canadian National Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_National_Railway "Canadian National Railway") (CN) companies, which operate [freight transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_freight_transport "Rail freight transport") across the province. Additional railfreight service in the province is provided by two shortline railways: the [Battle River Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_River_Railway "Battle River Railway") and [Forty Mile Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forty_Mile_Rail "Forty Mile Rail").

Passenger trains include [Via Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Via_Rail "Via Rail")'s *[Canadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_\(train\) "Canadian (train)")* (Toronto–Vancouver) and [Jasper–Prince Rupert trains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jasper%E2%80%93Prince_Rupert_train "Jasper–Prince Rupert train"), which use the CN mainline and pass through Jasper National Park and parallel the Yellowhead Highway during at least part of their routes. The [Rocky Mountaineer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountaineer "Rocky Mountaineer") operates two sections: one from Vancouver to Banff over CP tracks, and a section that travels over CN tracks to Jasper.

In 2024 Alberta's premier, Danielle Smith, announced a 15-year master plan to expand passenger rail in Alberta. This plan envisions rail services to [Lethbridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lethbridge "Lethbridge"), Medicine Hat, Banff, [Grande Prairie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grande_Prairie "Grande Prairie"), Fort McMurray, and most importantly an intercity rail service between Edmonton and [Calgary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calgary "Calgary"), as well as a [commuter rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commuter_rail "Commuter rail") systems in the respective cities. Ground-breaking was planned for 2027, according to Transportation Minister [Devin Dreeshen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devin_Dreeshen "Devin Dreeshen").[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-158)

### Road

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=33 "Edit section: Road")]

See also: [List of Alberta provincial highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Alberta_provincial_highways "List of Alberta provincial highways")

Alberta has over 473,000 km (294,000 mi) of highways and roads in its road network.[\[158\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-159) The main north–south corridor is [Highway 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Highway_2 "Alberta Highway 2"), which begins south of [Cardston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardston "Cardston") at the [Carway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carway,_Alberta "Carway, Alberta") border crossing and is part of the [CANAMEX Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CANAMEX_Corridor "CANAMEX Corridor"). Beginning at the [Coutts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coutts,_Alberta "Coutts, Alberta") border crossing and ending at Lethbridge, [Highway 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Highway_4 "Alberta Highway 4"), effectively extends [Interstate 15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_15 "Interstate 15") into Alberta and is the busiest United States gateway to the province. [Highway 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Highway_3 "Alberta Highway 3") joins Lethbridge to [Fort Macleod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Macleod "Fort Macleod") and links Highway 2 to Highway 4. Highway 2 travels north through Fort Macleod, Calgary, Red Deer, and Edmonton.[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-highwaychart-160)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/Alberta_Highway_22-Cowboy_Trail-_at_TransCanada_Highway.jpg/220px-Alberta_Highway_22-Cowboy_Trail-_at_TransCanada_Highway.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Alberta_Highway_22-Cowboy_Trail-_at_TransCanada_Highway.jpg)

[Highway 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Highway_1 "Alberta Highway 1") (the [Trans-Canada Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Canada_Highway "Trans-Canada Highway")) at [Alberta Highway 22](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Highway_22 "Alberta Highway 22") (Cowboy Trail).

North of Edmonton, the highway continues to [Athabasca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athabasca,_Alberta "Athabasca, Alberta"), then northwesterly along the south shore of [Lesser Slave Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesser_Slave_Lake "Lesser Slave Lake") into [High Prairie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Prairie "High Prairie"), north to [Peace River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_River,_Alberta "Peace River, Alberta"), west to [Fairview](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairview,_Alberta "Fairview, Alberta") and finally south to [Grande Prairie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grande_Prairie "Grande Prairie"), where it ends at an interchange with [Highway 43](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Highway_43 "Alberta Highway 43").[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-highwaychart-160) The section of Highway 2 between Calgary and Edmonton has been named the Queen Elizabeth II Highway to commemorate the visit of the monarch in 2005.[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-161) Highway 2 is supplemented by two more highways that run parallel to it: [Highway 22](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Highway_22 "Alberta Highway 22"), west of Highway 2, known as *Cowboy Trail*, and [Highway 21](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Highway_21 "Alberta Highway 21"), east of Highway 2. Highway 43 travels northwest into Grande Prairie and the [Peace River Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_River_Country "Peace River Country"). Travelling northeast from Edmonton, the [Highway 63](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Highway_63 "Alberta Highway 63") connects to Fort McMurrayand the Athabasca oil sands.[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-highwaychart-160)

Alberta has two main east–west corridors. The southern corridor, part of the [Trans-Canada Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Canada_Highway "Trans-Canada Highway") system, enters the province near Medicine Hat, runs westward through Calgary, and leaves Alberta through Banff National Park. The northern corridor, also part of the Trans-Canada network and known as the [Yellowhead Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellowhead_Highway "Yellowhead Highway") ([Highway 16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Highway_16 "Alberta Highway 16")), runs west from Lloydminster in eastern Alberta, through Edmonton and [Jasper National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jasper_National_Park "Jasper National Park") into British Columbia.[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-highwaychart-160) One of the most scenic drives is along the [Icefields Parkway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icefields_Parkway "Icefields Parkway"), which runs for 228 km (142 mi) between Jasper and Lake Louise, with mountain ranges and glaciers on either side of its entire length. A third corridor stretches across southern Alberta; [Highway 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Highway_3 "Alberta Highway 3") runs between [Crowsnest Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crowsnest_Pass "Crowsnest Pass") and Medicine Hat through Lethbridge and forms the eastern portion of the [Crowsnest Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crowsnest_Highway "Crowsnest Highway").[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-highwaychart-160) Another major corridor through central Alberta is [Highway 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Highway_11 "Alberta Highway 11") (also known as the [David Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Thompson_\(explorer\) "David Thompson (explorer)") Highway), which runs east from the [Saskatchewan River Crossing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saskatchewan_River_Crossing,_Alberta "Saskatchewan River Crossing, Alberta") in Banff National Park through [Rocky Mountain House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountain_House "Rocky Mountain House") and [Red Deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Deer,_Alberta "Red Deer, Alberta"), connecting with [Highway 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberta_Highway_12 "Alberta Highway 12"), 20 km (12 mi) west of [Stettler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stettler,_Alberta "Stettler, Alberta"). The highway connects many of the smaller towns in central Alberta with Calgary and Edmonton, as it crosses Highway 2 just west of Red Deer.[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-highwaychart-160)

Urban stretches of Alberta's major highways and freeways are often called *trails*. For example, Highway 2, the main north–south highway in the province, is called [Deerfoot Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deerfoot_Trail "Deerfoot Trail") as it passes through Calgary but becomes [Calgary Trail (southbound) and Gateway Boulevard (northbound)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calgary_Trail_%26_Gateway_Boulevard "Calgary Trail & Gateway Boulevard") as it enters Edmonton and then turns into [St. Albert Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Albert_Trail "St. Albert Trail") as it leaves Edmonton for the City of [St. Albert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Albert,_Alberta "St. Albert, Alberta"). Calgary, in particular, has a tradition of calling its largest urban [expressways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limited-access_road "Limited-access road") *trails* and naming many of them after prominent [First Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Nations_in_Canada "First Nations in Canada") individuals and tribes, such as [Crowchild Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crowchild_Trail "Crowchild Trail"), Deerfoot Trail, and [Stoney Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoney_Trail "Stoney Trail").[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Calgarymap-162)

## Friendship partners

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=34 "Edit section: Friendship partners")]

Alberta has relationships with many provinces, states, and other entities worldwide.[\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-163)

- [Gangwon-do](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gangwon_Province,_South_Korea "Gangwon Province, South Korea"), South Korea (1974)[\[163\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-164)
- [Hokkaido](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkaido "Hokkaido"), Japan (1980)
- [Heilongjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heilongjiang "Heilongjiang"), China (1981)
- [Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana "Montana"), United States (1985)
- [Tyumen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyumen "Tyumen"), Russia (1992)
- [Khanty–Mansi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanty-Mansi_Autonomous_Okrug "Khanty-Mansi Autonomous Okrug"), Russia (1995)
- [Yamalo-Nenets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamalo-Nenets_Autonomous_Okrug "Yamalo-Nenets Autonomous Okrug"), Russia (1997)
- [Jalisco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalisco "Jalisco"), Mexico (1999)
- [Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska "Alaska"), United States (2002)
- [Saxony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saxony "Saxony"), Germany (2002)
- [Ivano-Frankivsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivano-Frankivsk "Ivano-Frankivsk"), Ukraine (2004)
- [Lviv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lviv "Lviv"), Ukraine (2005)
- [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California "California"), United States (1997)[\[164\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-165)
- [Guangdong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangdong "Guangdong"), China (2017)

## Notes

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alberta\&action=edit\&section=36 "Edit section: Notes")]

1. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-134)** According to a 2018 CBC article, Albertans whose annual income is less than $25,000 pay the least income tax in Canada; those that earn about $50,000 "pay more than both Ontarians and British Columbians". Residents of British Columbia who earn about $75,000 pay $1,200 less in provincial taxes than those in Alberta. Albertans who earn about $100,000, "pay less than Ontarians but still more than people in B.C." Alberta taxpayers who earn $250,000 a year or more, pay $4,000 less in provincial taxes than someone with a similar income in B.C. and "about $18,000 less than in Quebec."

120. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-120)** Eisen, Ben (March 31, 2018). Retrieved September 22, 2018. "When Rachel Notley's NDP shook Alberta's political landscape by winning a majority government in 2015, the similarities to Ontario's Bob Rae NDP government in the 1990s were striking. Both cases marked the first NDP government in provincial history, and both brought an end to Progressive Conservative dynasties (though in the case of Ontario, the beginning of the end had come a few years earlier when David Peterson formed a minority Liberal government)."
